[Narrator] Maisie strode forward swiftly, a hand in her pocket and her chin in the air. [/]
[Narrator] Dick followed with a small pin - fire revolver. [/]
[Narrator] The children had discovered that their lives would be unendurable without pistol - practice. [/]
[Narrator] After much forethought and self - denial, Dick had saved seven shillings and sixpence, the price of a badly constructed Belgian revolver. [/]
[Narrator] Maisie could only contribute half a crown to the syndicate for the purchase of a hundred cartridges. [/]
[Maisie] “ You can save better than I can, Dick, ” she explained; “ I like nice things to eat, and it does n’t matter to you. [/]
[Maisie] Besides, boys ought to do these things. ” [/]
[Narrator] Dick grumbled a little at the arrangement, but went out and made the purchase, which the children were then on their way to test. [/]
[Narrator] Revolvers did not lie in the scheme of their daily life as decreed for them by the guardian who was incorrectly supposed to stand in the place of a mother to these two orphans. [/]
[Narrator] Dick had been under her care for six years, during which time she had made her profit of the allowances supposed to be expended on his clothes, and, partly through thoughtlessness, partly through a natural desire to pain, —she was a widow of some years anxious to marry again, —had made his days burdensome on his young shoulders. [/]
[Narrator] Where he had looked for love, she gave him first aversion and then hate. [/]
[Narrator] Where he growing older had sought a little sympathy, she gave him ridicule. [/]
[Narrator] The many hours that she could spare from the ordering of her small house she devoted to what she called the home - training of Dick Heldar. [/]
[Narrator] Her religion, manufactured in the main by her own intelligence and a keen study of the Scriptures, was an aid to her in this matter. [/]
[Narrator] At such times as she herself was not personally displeased with Dick, she left him to understand that he had a heavy account to settle with his Creator; wherefore Dick learned to loathe his God as intensely as he loathed Mrs. Jennett; and this is not a wholesome frame of mind for the young. [/]
[Narrator] Since she chose to regard him as a hopeless liar, when dread of pain drove him to his first untruth, he naturally developed into a liar, but an economical and self - contained one, never throwing away the least unnecessary fib, and never hesitating at the blackest, were it only plausible, that might make his life a little easier. [/]
[Narrator] The treatment taught him at least the power of living alone, —a power that was of service to him when he went to a public school and the boys laughed at his clothes, which were poor in quality and much mended. [/]
[Narrator] In the holidays he returned to the teachings of Mrs. Jennett, and, that the chain of discipline might not be weakened by association with the world, was generally beaten, on one account or another, before he had been twelve hours under her roof. [/]
[Narrator] The autumn of one year brought him a companion in bondage, a long - haired, gray - eyed little atom, as self - contained as himself, who moved about the house silently and for the first few weeks spoke only to the goat that was her chiefest friend on earth and lived in the back - garden. [/]
[Narrator] Mrs. Jennett objected to the goat on the grounds that he was un - Christian, —which he certainly was. [/]
[TheAtom] “ Then, ” said the atom, choosing her words very deliberately, “ I shall write to my lawyer - peoples and tell them that you are a very bad woman. [/]
[TheAtom] Amomma is mine, mine, mine! ” [/]
[Narrator] Mrs. Jennett made a movement to the hall, where certain umbrellas and canes stood in a rack. [/]
[Narrator] The atom understood as clearly as Dick what this meant. [/]
[MrsJennett] “ I have been beaten before, ” she said, still in the same passionless voice; “ I have been beaten worse than you can ever beat me. [/]
[MrsJennett] If you beat me I shall write to my lawyer - peoples and tell them that you do not give me enough to eat. [/]
[MrsJennett] I am not afraid of you. ” [/]
[Narrator] Mrs. Jennett did not go into the hall, and the atom, after a pause to assure herself that all danger of war was past, went out, to weep bitterly on Amomma ’s neck. [/]
[Narrator] Dick learned to know her as Maisie, and at first mistrusted her profoundly, for he feared that she might interfere with the small liberty of action left to him. [/]
[Narrator] She did not, however; and she volunteered no friendliness until Dick had taken the first steps. [/]
[Narrator] Long before the holidays were over, the stress of punishment shared in common drove the children together, if it were only to play into each other ’s hands as they prepared lies for Mrs. Jennett ’s use. [/]
[Maisie] When Dick returned to school, Maisie whispered, “ Now I shall be all alone to take care of myself; but, ” and she nodded her head bravely, “ I can do it. [/]
[Maisie] You promised to send Amomma a grass collar. [/]
[Maisie] Send it soon. ” [/]
[Narrator] A week later she asked for that collar by return of post, and was not pleased when she learned that it took time to make. [/]
[Narrator] When at last Dick forwarded the gift, she forgot to thank him for it. [/]
[Narrator] Many holidays had come and gone since that day, and Dick had grown into a lanky hobbledehoy more than ever conscious of his bad clothes. [/]
[Narrator] Not for a moment had Mrs. Jennett relaxed her tender care of him, but the average canings of a public school — Dick fell under punishment about three times a month — filled him with contempt for her powers. [/]
[Dick] “ She does n’t hurt, ” he explained to Maisie, who urged him to rebellion, “ and she is kinder to you after she has whacked me. ” [/]
[Narrator] Dick shambled through the days unkempt in body and savage in soul, as the smaller boys of the school learned to know, for when the spirit moved him he would hit them, cunningly and with science. [/]
[Narrator] The same spirit made him more than once try to tease Maisie, but the girl refused to be made unhappy. [/]

[Narrator] Rapid had followed rapid, rock rock, and island - group island - group, till the rank and file had long since lost all count of direction and very nearly of time. [/]

[Narrator] Before them lay the Nile, and at the other end of it was one Gordon, fighting for the dear life, in a town called Khartoum. [/]
[Narrator] There were columns of British troops in the desert, or in one of the many deserts; there were yet more columns waiting to embark on the river; there were fresh drafts waiting at Assioot and Assuan; there were lies and rumours running over the face of the hopeless land from Suakin to the Sixth Cataract, and men supposed generally that there must be some one in authority to direct the general scheme of the many movements. [/]
[TheSoldiers] The duty of that particular river - column was to keep the whale - boats afloat in the water, to avoid trampling on the villagers ’ crops when the gangs “ tracked ’ the boats with lines thrown from midstream, to get as much sleep and food as was possible, and, above all, to press on without delay in the teeth of the churning Nile. [/]
[Narrator] With the soldiers sweated and toiled the correspondents of the newspapers, and they were almost as ignorant as their companions. [/]

[Narrator] The Soudan campaign was a picturesque one, and lent itself to vivid word - painting. [/]
[ASpecial] Now and again a “ Special ’ managed to get slain, —which was not altogether a disadvantage to the paper that employed him, —and more often the hand - to - hand nature of the fighting allowed of miraculous escapes which were worth telegraphing home at eighteenpence the word. [/]
[ASpecial] There were many correspondents with many corps and columns, —from the veterans who had followed on the heels of the cavalry that occupied Cairo in ’ 82, what time Arabi Pasha called himself king, who had seen the first miserable work round Suakin when the sentries were cut up nightly and the scrub swarmed with spears, to youngsters jerked into the business at the end of a telegraph - wire to take the places of their betters killed or invalided. [/]
[Narrator] Among the seniors — those who knew every shift and change in the perplexing postal arrangements, the value of the seediest, weediest Egyptian garron offered for sale in Cairo or Alexandria, who could talk a telegraph - clerk into amiability and soothe the ruffled vanity of a newly appointed staff - officer when press regulations became burdensome — was the man in the flannel shirt, the black - browed Torpenhow. [Narrator] So it came to pass that, after some purchase of horse - flesh and arrangements financial and political, Dick was made free of the New and Honourable Fraternity of war correspondents, who all possess the inalienable right of doing as much work as they can and getting as much for it as Providence and their owners shall please. [/]
[Narrator] To these things are added in time, if the brother be worthy, the power of glib speech that neither man nor woman can resist when a meal or a bed is in question, the eye of a horse - cope, the skill of a cook, the constitution of a bullock, the digestion of an ostrich, and an infinite adaptability to all circumstances. [/]
[Narrator] But many die before they attain to this degree, and the past - masters in the craft appear for the most part in dress - clothes when they are in England, and thus their glory is hidden from the multitude. [/]
[Narrator] Dick followed Torpenhow wherever the latter ’s fancy chose to lead him, and between the two they managed to accomplish some work that almost satisfied themselves. [/]

[Narrator] The little hundred - and - fifty - pound camel - guns posted at one corner of the square opened the ball as the square moved forward by its right to get possession of a knoll of rising ground. [/]
[Narrator] All had fought in this manner many times before, and there was no novelty in the entertainment; always the same hot and stifling formation, the smell of dust and leather, the same boltlike rush of the enemy, the same pressure on the weakest side, the few minutes of hand - to - hand scuffle, and then the silence of the desert, broken only by the yells of those whom their handful of cavalry attempted to pursue. [/]
[Narrator] They had become careless. [/]
[Narrator] The camel - guns spoke at intervals, and the square slouched forward amid the protesting of the camels. [/]
[Narrator] Then came the attack of three thousand men who had not learned from books that it is impossible for troops in close order to attack against breech - loading fire. [/]
[Narrator] A few dropping shots heralded their approach, and a few horsemen led, but the bulk of the force was naked humanity, mad with rage, and armed with the spear and the sword. [/]
[Narrator] The instinct of the desert, where there is always much war, told them that the right flank of the square was the weakest, for they swung clear of the front. [/]
[Narrator] The camel - guns shelled them as they passed and opened for an instant lanes through their midst, most like those quick - closing vistas in a Kentish hop - garden seen when the train races by at full speed; and the infantry fire, held till the opportune moment, dropped them in close - packing hundreds. [/]
[Narrator] No civilised troops in the world could have endured the hell through which they came, the living leaping high to avoid the dying who clutched at their heels, the wounded cursing and staggering forward, till they fell — a torrent black as the sliding water above a mill - dam — full on the right flank of the square. [/]
[Narrator] Then the line of the dusty troops and the faint blue desert sky overhead went out in rolling smoke, and the little stones on the heated ground and the tinder - dry clumps of scrub became matters of surpassing interest, for men measured their agonised retreat and recovery by these things, counting mechanically and hewing their way back to chosen pebble and branch. [/]
[Narrator] There was no semblance of any concerted fighting. [/]
[Narrator] For aught the men knew, the enemy might be attempting all four sides of the square at once. [/]
[Narrator] Their business was to destroy what lay in front of them, to bayonet in the back those who passed over them, and, dying, to drag down the slayer till he could be knocked on the head by some avenging gun - butt. [/]
[Narrator] Dick waited with Torpenhow and a young doctor till the stress grew unendurable. [/]

[Narrator] Dick loitered in Cairo, Alexandria, Ismailia, and Port Said, —especially Port Said. [/]

[Narrator] Dick established himself in quarters more riotous than respectable. [/]

[Narrator] Three turns of the wheel left him richer by twenty pounds, and he went down to the shipping to make friends with the captain of a decayed cargo - steamer, who landed him in London with fewer pounds in his pocket than he cared to think about. [/]
[Narrator] A thin gray fog hung over the city, and the streets were very cold; for summer was in England. [/]
[Dick] “ It ’s a cheerful wilderness, and it has n’t the knack of altering much, ” Dick thought, as he tramped from the Docks westward. [/]
[Dick] “ Now, what must I do? ” [/]
[Narrator] The packed houses gave no answer. [/]
[Narrator] Dick looked down the long lightless streets and at the appalling rush of traffic. [/]
[Dick] “ Oh, you rabbit - hutches! ” said he, addressing a row of highly respectable semi - detached residences. [/]
[Dick] “ Do you know what you ’ve got to do later on? [/]
[Dick] You have to supply me with men - servants and maid - servants, ”—here he smacked his lips, —“and the peculiar treasure of kings. [/]

[Narrator] He stepped forward energetically; he saw that one of his shoes was burst at the side. [/]
[Narrator] As he stooped to make investigations, a man jostled him into the gutter. [/]
[Dick] “ All right, ” he said. [/]
[AMan] “ That ’s another nick in the score. [/]
[AMan] I ’ll jostle you later on. ” [/]
[Narrator] Good clothes and boots are not cheap, and Dick left his last shop with the certainty that he would be respectably arrayed for a time, but with only fifty shillings in his pocket. [/]
[Narrator] He returned to streets by the Docks, and lodged himself in one room, where the sheets on the bed were almost audibly marked in case of theft, and where nobody seemed to go to bed at all. [/]

[Dick] There ’s a big deal coming. ” [/]
[Narrator] So he departed, making no promises, to his one little room by the Docks. [/]
[Narrator] And that day was the seventh of the month, and that month, he reckoned with awful distinctness, had thirty - one days in it! [/]
[Narrator] It is not easy for a man of catholic tastes and healthy appetites to exist for twenty - four days on fifty shillings. [/]
[Narrator] Nor is it cheering to begin the experiment alone in all the loneliness of London. [/]
[Narrator] Dick paid seven shillings a week for his lodging, which left him rather less than a shilling a day for food and drink. [/]
[Narrator] Naturally, his first purchase was of the materials of his craft; he had been without them too long. [/]
[Narrator] Half a day ’s investigations and comparison brought him to the conclusion that sausages and mashed potatoes, twopence a plate, were the best food. [/]
[Narrator] Now, sausages once or twice a week for breakfast are not unpleasant. [/]
[Narrator] As lunch, even, with mashed potatoes, they become monotonous. [/]
[Narrator] At dinner they are impertinent. [/]
[Narrator] At the end of three days Dick loathed sausages, and, going forth, pawned his watch to revel on sheep ’s head, which is not as cheap as it looks, owing to the bones and the gravy. [/]
[Narrator] Then he returned to sausages and mashed potatoes. [/]
[Narrator] Then he confined himself entirely to mashed potatoes for a day, and was unhappy because of pain in his inside. [/]
[Narrator] Then he pawned his waistcoat and his tie, and thought regretfully of money thrown away in times past. [/]
[Narrator] There are few things more edifying unto Art than the actual belly - pinch of hunger, and Dick in his few walks abroad, —he did not care for exercise; it raised desires that could not be satisfied — found himself dividing mankind into two classes, —those who looked as if they might give him something to eat, and those who looked otherwise. [/]
[Dick] “ I never knew what I had to learn about the human face before, ” he thought; and, as a reward for his humility, Providence caused a cab - driver at a sausage - shop where Dick fed that night to leave half eaten a great chunk of bread. [/]

[/]

[Narrator] CHAPTER XIII [/]
[Narrator] The sun went down an hour ago, I wonder if I face towards home; If I lost my way in the light of day How shall I find it now night is come? — _ Old Song _. [/]

[Narrator] Summer had come upon Vitry - sur - Marne and parched it to the bone. [/]
[Narrator] The grass was dry - burnt in the meadows, the clay by the bank of the river was caked to brick, the roadside flowers were long since dead, and the roses in the garden hung withered on their stalks. [/]
[Narrator] The heat in the little low bedroom under the eaves was almost intolerable. [/]
[Narrator] The very moonlight on the wall of Kami ’s studio across the road seemed to make the night hotter, and the shadow of the big bell - handle by the closed gate cast a bar of inky black that caught Maisie ’s eye and annoyed her. [/]
[Maisie] “ Horrid thing! [/]

[Maisie] “ And the gate is n’t in the middle of the wall, either. [/]
[Maisie] I never noticed that before. ” [/]
[Narrator] Maisie was hard to please at that hour. [/]
[Narrator] First, the heat of the past few weeks had worn her down; secondly, her work, and particularly the study of a female head intended to represent the Melancolia and not finished in time for the Salon, was unsatisfactory; thirdly, Kami had said as much two days before; fourthly, —but so completely fourthly that it was hardly worth thinking about, —Dick, her property, had not written to her for more than six weeks. [/]
[Narrator] She was angry with the heat, with Kami, and with her work, but she was exceedingly angry with Dick. [/]
[Narrator] She had written to him three times, —each time proposing a fresh treatment of her Melancolia. [/]
[Narrator] Dick had taken no notice of these communications. [/]
[Narrator] She had resolved to write no more. [/]
[Narrator] When she returned to England in the autumn — for her pride ’s sake she could not return earlier — she would speak to him. [/]
[Narrator] She missed the Sunday afternoon conferences more than she cared to admit. [/]
[Dick] All that Kami said was, “ _ Continuez, mademoiselle, continuez toujours _, ” and he had been repeating the wearisome counsel through the hot summer, exactly like a cicada, —an old gray cicada in a black alpaca coat, white trousers, and a huge felt hat. [/]
[Narrator] But Dick had tramped masterfully up and down her little studio north of the cool green London park, and had said things ten times worse than _ continuez _, before he snatched the brush out of her hand and showed her where the error lay. [/]
[Narrator] His last letter, Maisie remembered, contained some trivial advice about not sketching in the sun or drinking water at wayside farmhouses; and he had said that not once, but three times, —as if he did not know that Maisie could take care of herself. [/]
[Narrator] But what was he doing, that he could not trouble to write? [/]
[Narrator] A murmur of voices in the road made her lean from the window. [/]
[Narrator] A cavalryman of the little garrison in the town was talking to Kami ’s cook. [/]

[Narrator] The cook ’s cap cast deep shadows on her face, which was close to the conscript ’s. [/]
[Narrator] He slid his arm round her waist, and there followed the sound of a kiss. [/]

[Narrator] He could not! [/]

[Narrator] The rose continued to nod in the futile way peculiar to flowers. [/]
[Narrator] There was no earthly reason why Dick should not disport himself as he chose, except that he was called by Providence, which was Maisie, to assist Maisie in her work. [/]
[Narrator] And her work was the preparation of pictures that went sometimes to English provincial exhibitions, as the notices in the scrap - book proved, and that were invariably rejected by the Salon when Kami was plagued into allowing her to send them up. [/]

[Narrator] Exactly the same way. [/]

[Narrator] Dick had told her that he had worked ten years to understand his craft. [/]
[Narrator] She had worked ten years, and ten years were nothing. [/]
[Narrator] Dick had said that ten years were nothing, —but that was in regard to herself only. [/]
[Narrator] He had said — this very man who could not find time to write — that he would wait ten years for her, and that she was bound to come back to him sooner or later. [/]

[Narrator] Torpenhow had been long away; there was no more rioting in the chambers, and Dick had settled down to his new life, which he was weak enough to consider nothing better than death. [/]
[Narrator] It is hard to live alone in the dark, confusing the day and night; dropping to sleep through sheer weariness at mid - day, and rising restless in the chill of the dawn. [/]
[Narrator] At first Dick, on his awakenings, would grope along the corridors of the chambers till he heard some one snore. [/]
[Narrator] Then he would know that the day had not yet come, and return wearily to his bedroom. [/]
[Narrator] Later he learned not to stir till there was a noise and movement in the house and Mr. Beeton advised him to get up. [/]
[Narrator] Once dressed — and dressing, now that Torpenhow was away, was a lengthy business, because collars, ties, and the like hid themselves in far corners of the room, and search meant head - beating against chairs and trunks — once dressed, there was nothing whatever to do except to sit still and brood till the three daily meals came. [/]
[Narrator] Centuries separated breakfast from lunch and lunch from dinner, and though a man prayed for hundreds of years that his mind might be taken from him, God would never hear. [/]
[Narrator] Rather the mind was quickened and the revolving thoughts ground against each other as millstones grind when there is no corn between; and yet the brain would not wear out and give him rest. [/]
[Narrator] It continued to think, at length, with imagery and all manner of reminiscences. [/]
[Narrator] It recalled Maisie and past success, reckless travels by land and sea, the glory of doing work and feeling that it was good, and suggested all that might have happened had the eyes only been faithful to their duty. [/]
[Narrator] When thinking ceased through sheer weariness, there poured into Dick ’s soul tide on tide of overwhelming, purposeless fear — dread of starvation always, terror lest the unseen ceiling should crush down upon him, fear of fire in the chambers and a louse ’s death in red flame, and agonies of fiercer horror that had nothing to do with any fear of death. [/]
[Narrator] Then Dick bowed his head, and clutching the arms of his chair fought with his sweating self till the tinkle of plates told him that something to eat was being set before him. [/]
[Narrator] Mr. Beeton would bring the meal when he had time to spare, and Dick learned to hang upon his speech, which dealt with badly fitted gas - plugs, waste - pipes out of repair, little tricks for driving picture - nails into walls, and the sins of the charwoman or the housemaids. [/]
[Narrator] In the lack of better things the small gossip of a servant ’’ hall becomes immensely interesting, and the screwing of a washer on a tap an event to be talked over for days. [/]
[Narrator] Once or twice a week, too, Mr. Beeton would take Dick out with him when he went marketing in the morning to haggle with tradesmen over fish, lamp - wicks, mustard, tapioca, and so forth, while Dick rested his weight first on one foot and then on the other and played aimlessly with the tins and string - ball on the counter. [/]
[Narrator] Then they would perhaps meet one of Mr. Beeton ’s friends, and Dick, standing aside a little, would hold his peace till Mr. Beeton was willing to go on again. [/]
[Narrator] The life did not increase his self - respect. [/]
[Narrator] He abandoned shaving as a dangerous exercise, and being shaved in a barber ’s shop meant exposure of his infirmity. [/]
[Narrator] He could not see that his clothes were properly brushed, and since he had never taken any care of his personal appearance he became every known variety of sloven. [/]
[Narrator] A blind man can not deal with cleanliness till he has been some months used to the darkness. [/]
[Narrator] If he demand attendance and grow angry at the want of it, he must assert himself and stand upright. [/]
[Narrator] Then the meanest menial can see that he is blind and, therefore, of no consequence. [/]
[Narrator] A wise man will keep his eyes on the floor and sit still. [/]
[Narrator] For amusement he may pick coal lump by lump out of the scuttle with the tongs and pile it in a little heap in the fender, keeping count of the lumps, which must all be put back again, one by one and very carefully. [/]
[Narrator] He may set himself sums if he cares to work them out; he may talk to himself or to the cat if she chooses to visit him; and if his trade has been that of an artist, he may sketch in the air with his forefinger; but that is too much like drawing a pig with the eyes shut. [/]
[Narrator] He may go to his bookshelves and count his books, ranging them in order of their size; or to his wardrobe and count his shirts, laying them in piles of two or three on the bed, as they suffer from frayed cuffs or lost buttons. [/]
[Narrator] Even this entertainment wearies after a time; and all the times are very, very long. [/]

[Narrator] Then God was very kind: a deep sleep of weariness came upon him just as he would have thought of Maisie, and he slept till the steamer had cleared the mouth of the Thames and was lifting to the pulse of the Channel. [/]
[Narrator] The rattle of the engines, the reek of oil and paint, and a very familiar sound in the next cabin roused him to his new inheritance. [/]
[Dick] “ Oh, it ’s good to be alive again! ” [/]
[Narrator] He yawned, stretched himself vigorously, and went on deck to be told that they were almost abreast of the lights of Brighton. [/]
[Narrator] This is no more open water than Trafalgar Square is a common; the free levels begin at Ushant; but none the less Dick could feel the healing of the sea at work upon him already. [/]
[Narrator] A boisterous little cross - swell swung the steamer disrespectfully by the nose; and one wave breaking far aft spattered the quarterdeck and the pile of new deck - chairs. [/]
[Narrator] He heard the foam fall with the clash of broken glass, was stung in the face by a cupful, and sniffing luxuriously, felt his way to the smoking - room by the wheel. [/]
[Narrator] There a strong breeze found him, blew his cap off and left him bareheaded in the doorway, and the smoking - room steward, understanding that he was a voyager of experience, said that the weather would be stiff in the chops off the Channel and more than half a gale in the Bay. [/]
[Narrator] These things fell as they were foretold, and Dick enjoyed himself to the utmost. [/]
[Narrator] It is allowable and even necessary at sea to lay firm hold upon tables, stanchions, and ropes in moving from place to place. [/]
[Narrator] On land the man who feels with his hands is patently blind. [/]
[Narrator] At sea even a blind man who is not sea - sick can jest with the doctor over the weakness of his fellows. [/]
[Narrator] Dick told the doctor many tales — and these are coin of more value than silver if properly handled — smoked with him till unholy hours of the night, and so won his short - lived regard that he promised Dick a few hours of his time when they came to Port Said. [/]
[Narrator] And the sea roared or was still as the winds blew, and the engines sang their song day and night, and the sun grew stronger day by day, and Tom the Lascar barber shaved Dick of a morning under the opened hatch - grating where the cool winds blew, and the awnings were spread and the passengers made merry, and at last they came to Port Said. [Narrator] There was colour, light, and motion, without which no man has much pleasure in living. [/]
[Narrator] This night there remained for him only one more journey through the darkness that never lifts to tell a man how far he has travelled. [/]
[Narrator] Then he would grip Torpenhow ’s hand again — Torpenhow, who was alive and strong, and lived in the midst of the action that had once made the reputation of a man called Dick Heldar: not in the least to be confused with the blind, bewildered vagabond who seemed to answer to the same name. [/]
[Narrator] Yes, he would find Torpenhow, and come as near to the old life as might be. [/]
[Narrator] Afterwards he would forget everything: Bessie, who had wrecked the Melancolia and so nearly wrecked his life; Beeton, who lived in a strange unreal city full of tin - tacks and gas - plugs and matters that no men needed; that irrational being who had offered him love and loyalty for nothing, but had not signed her name; and most of all Maisie, who, from her own point of view, was undeniably right in all she did, but oh, at this distance, so tantalisingly fair. [/]
[Narrator] George ’s hand on his arm pulled him back to the situation. [/]
[George] “ And what now? ” said George. [/]
[George] “ Oh yes of course. [/]
[George] What now? Take me to the camel - men. [/]
[George] Take me to where the scouts sit when they come in from the desert. [/]
[George] They sit by their camels, and the camels eat grain out of a black blanket held up at the corners, and the men eat by their side just like camels. [/]
[George] Take me there! ” [/]
[Narrator] The camp was rough and rutty, and Dick stumbled many times over the stumps of scrub. [/]
[Narrator] The scouts were sitting by their beasts, as Dick knew they would. [/]
[Narrator] The light of the dung - fires flickered on their bearded faces, and the camels bubbled and mumbled beside them at rest. [/]
[Narrator] It was no part of Dick ’s policy to go into the desert with a convoy of supplies. [/]
[Narrator] That would lead to impertinent questions, and since a blind non - combatant is not needed at the front, he would probably be forced to return to Suakin. [/]
[Narrator] He must go up alone, and go immediately. [/]
[Dick] “ Now for one last bluff — the biggest of all, ” he said. [/]
[Dick] “ Peace be with you, brethren! ” [/]
[Narrator] The watchful George steered him to the circle of the nearest fire. [/]
[Narrator] The heads of the camel - sheiks bowed gravely, and the camels, scenting a European, looked sideways curiously like brooding hens, half ready to get to their feet. [/]
[Dick] “ A beast and a driver to go to the fighting line to - night, ” said Dick. [/]

[Dick] “ A Bisharin without saddle - galls. [/]
[Dick] Therefore no charge of thine, shock - head. ” [/]
[Narrator] Two or three minutes passed. [/]
[Narrator] Then—“We be knee - haltered for the night. [/]
[TheSheik] There is no going out from the camp. ” [/]
[TheSheik] “ Not for money? ” “ H’m! [/]
[TheSheik] Ah! [/]
[TheSheik] English money? ” Another depressing interval of silence. [/]

[Narrator] This was royal payment, and the sheik, who knew that he would get his commission on this deposit, stirred in Dick ’s behalf. [/]
[Dick] “ For scarcely one night ’s journey — fifty pounds. [/]
[Dick] Land and wells and good trees and wives to make a man content for the rest of his days. [/]

[TheSheik] “ I, ” said a voice. [/]
[Narrator] “ I will go — but there is no going from the camp. ” “ Fool! [/]

[TheLatter] Twenty - five pounds and another twenty - five pounds. [/]
[TheLatter] But the beast must be a good Bisharin; I will take no baggage - camel. ” [/]
[Narrator] Then the bargaining began, and at the end of half an hour the first deposit was paid over to the sheik, who talked in low tones to the driver. [/]

[TheMen] “ That is well. [/]

[Narrator] His head dropped on his chest and he tried to think, but the tenor of his thoughts was broken because he was very sleepy. [/]

[Narrator] He had committed some crime as bad as Sabbath - breaking, and she had locked him up in his bedroom. [/]

[Narrator] Dick could think no longer. [/]

[Narrator] From the safe distance of London he was watching himself thus employed, —watching critically. [/]
